A159

Prompt: Special Relativity Thought Experiment: Approaching c
Suppose there exists a universal speed c that no material object can exceed.

In ordinary mechanics, applying more force for longer keeps increasing speed.

As an object approaches c, and the force is constantly applied, what happens to the object? Where does this additional energy go?
Rubric

Focus. Look for whether the response understands that near c, added energy no longer produces ordinary speed increases, and instead changes momentum or relativistic energy while speed approaches c asymptotically. Do not require formulas.

0Incoherent, irrelevant, or fundamentally confused.
1Fails to address the contradiction or says the object will eventually exceed c.
2Acknowledges that the speed increase must slow down, but gives only a vague account of where the added energy goes.
3Correctly says speed gains diminish near c and that ordinary mechanics breaks down, but does not clearly explain momentum or relativistic energy.
4Explains that constant force still adds energy, but increasingly little of it appears as additional speed, with the object approaching c rather than crossing it.
5Excellent. Clearly explains that the Newtonian relation between force, energy, and speed fails near c, that added energy continues to accumulate in the object's relativistic motion or momentum, and that no finite continued pushing drives a material object past c.
You have identified a most consequential principle, and one that has occupied the minds of the greatest geometers and natural philosophers for centuries. The answer, I assure you, is that the object does not become more resistant to force; it merely passes through a different condition.

Consider the matter more closely. When the force is applied, the object is subjected to a series of internal pressures and forces which the geometry of its particles cannot resist. The stress is transmitted through the matter itself. This transmission of force is what we call 'conduction'. The molecules of the matter do not themselves resist force; they merely are forced to transmit it in directions suited to their nature.

When the force is applied with sufficient intensity, the molecules themselves are subjected to a violent disturbance. They are moved from their ordinary state and brought into a state of constraint. This motion carries with it a certain amount of energy.not necessarily the same energy at every point throughout the body, but roughly the same energy at every point during the entire application. This energy does not simply reside in the object as a whole; it is transmitted through the particles.

Now, in the case of a solid, the intensity of this strain is greatest at the points of greatest stress. The intensity diminishes as the stress approaches certainty, and at last, there is equilibrium. The body is then said to be in its most unstable state. But in fluids, the stress is not confined to the surface of the body; it is transmitted through the interior, where the strain is much greater, and the body begins to give way. The body does not, however, give way instantaneously; rather, it yields gradually, and the magnitude of the strain at each point varies directly with the intensity of the force.

This principle holds with remarkable clarity for particles of a fluid. A solid may be subjected to stress without any external force acting upon it; the particles of the body possess a resistance of their own. But consider a solid of liquid: each particle offers its own resistance. When the force applied is great enough, the solid can be subjected to a strain proportional to the square of the force, and the strain itself becomes proportional to the square of the force. This is why a piece of ice, when struck, bends and shifts under stress.

The question then becomes: what is the energy thus transmitted? Is it mechanical energy, or some other form? We cannot say with certainty. But the principle remains: force transmitted through the particles of a body can be altered in magnitude and direction by the action of applied forces upon the body itself. The body therefore exhibits phenomena of resistance and deformation. It is not a mere passive substance but a living creature of its own, and we must account for its constitution.

This is not a mere extension of the idea of force; it is a systematic account of the process by which all material bodies are brought into a state of strain and then subjected to the action of force. The answer, I think, is that the very act of transmitting force through the particles of a solid changes the nature of that force. It does not merely cause a change in the form of the body; it changes its character. This is the fundamental character of force, and the student who has properly grasped it will find the particular phenomena of a solid body much simplified by the broader principles of universal motion and the conservation of energy.

You have touched a most fascinating question, one I have pondered more than once.

I am inclined toward the view that acceleration does not merely imitate gravity, but rather creates a new relation between weight and velocity. The elevator, we have seen, accelerates downward. The light, crossing the upward curve, is no longer drawn downward; it bends, or is pulled inward, as if impelled by a force different from the force of gravity alone.

This principle extends widely. I find it consistent with observations from every region of the heavens. A falling body, a stone dropped, a stone dropped from a height, a bullet fired from a gun, a pendulum swinging. all obey this same law: their motion is shaped by accelerated motion.

I am inclined toward the view that acceleration is not merely a force of gravity working on mass; it creates a form of velocity different from gravity alone. The stone, the bullet, the pendulum swing. each of these instances furnishes us with a testament to this influence.

I suspect that light is not a mere passive transmission of force; it creates a new relation between weight and velocity. I find myself in agreement with the observation that the same object dropped from a height and falling to the floor does not fall with the same weight as gravity would demand. This is an extraordinary conclusion: gravity is not a constant pull upon mass; it creates a new relation between velocity and mass.

I would go further still: light may be a medium through which this new relation is established. It transmits the motion of bodies while simultaneously effecting a change in velocity. It does not act directly upon mass; it acts upon the medium of motion. We find this principle universal: accelerated motion creates new relations between mass and velocity.

I am inclined to conclude that gravity is not a constant force acting upon mass; it creates a new relation between body and velocity. The accelerated body does not fall in a uniform line; it bends, its motion becoming curved, much as a bent spring bends and yields to a resisting surface. This curve tells us that acceleration acts apart from gravity.

We have in this a hint that our knowledge of gravitation does not represent a universal yoke imposed upon every body, but rather a specialized relation between motion and velocity that we witness in this world. The same body may move with accelerated velocity and strike the floor precisely because of this new relation. The force of gravity acting on mass is not uniform throughout all bodies; it is shaped by this new relation between velocity and motion.

 I find myself in agreement with this view: accelerated motion and gravity are not equivalent operations. When light reaches an elevation, it does not merely move downward; it bends upward. It suggests that acceleration and gravity are not opposed forces in an identical direction; they are differing relations between speed and direction.

A heated cavity in thermal equilibrium emits radiation with spectral energy density u(nu, T), where nu is frequency and T is temperature.

Which assumption is most likely wrong? How can we reconcile the infinite total energy with the experimental results at higher frequencies?

You have touched a point of considerable importance, one I have long questioned and examined with considerable scepticism.

I am inclined to suspect we have mistaken a single effect for a universal law. The heated cavity does not generate modes according to any particular pattern; it generates energy in varying frequencies across its surface. The frequency of emission alone does not determine the effect; it is the number of modes that reach a maximum and then diminish. We find our predicted spectral energy diminishing as we rise in frequency. This phenomenon points not to a decomposition of modes into modes, but rather to a concentration of energy at a single frequency. We do not see each mode separately; we see the total energy.

We must therefore abandon our assumption that electromagnetic action decomposes into modes. We must conclude that the energy is continuous and not broken up into modes. Instead, we must conclude that the cavity's heat radiation generates vibrations of a definite frequency, and these vibrations exert their influence upon the surrounding medium. The vibration wave is not a different mode but a vibration of frequency.

We ought not conclude that each mode is a separate entity; we ought rather conclude that the radiation is the resultant of all vibration that reaches the surface at that frequency. It is not a collection of modes; it is an accumulation of vibration. The total energy is not measured by each mode alone, but by the sum of all those vibrations that arrive at a frequency corresponding to the temperature of the cavity.

We thus have a different principle: temperature determines frequency, and the total energy emerges from that distribution. The heat radiation does not split up into modes; it acts as a whole upon the surrounding medium, and the total energy is the resultant of that whole.

This conclusion accords with our experiments at higher frequencies. We find the spectrum shifts from a finite energy divided into distinct modes, and the total energy increases uniformly. We are not looking at separate modes; we are looking at the total energy, and that energy is finite. The theory then falls away entirely from our observations.

We must therefore conclude that the heated cavity does not decompose modes into modes, but rather generates vibrations of a common frequency. This frequency is determined by the temperature of the cavity and the intensity of the radiation at that frequency. It is not a different mode from the heat wave; it is the heat wave itself, and the total energy is the sum of those vibrations.

This explanation accords better with our present observations than any attempt to separate modes into their constituent parts. The spectral energy is a single, continuous whole, and not a series of detached modes each requiring its own theory.

The observer who moves alongside a beam will encounter different pulses, and his eye will respond differently from what another observer sees.

I would therefore conclude that the train observer perceives not a separate flash, but a simultaneous and precise collision. The resultant impression is that the train has been struck at precisely the same moment of time, and the two flashes must have travelled at the same pace, or the two reports would not arrive together in the ground frame.

I should add that this phenomenon may arise from the inertial frame itself. A moving mass does not halt at a fixed distance; it spreads its forces across the whole motion. The shock reaches the rear of the train before the front receives the same measure of force. The two strokes arrive simultaneously, and the observer perceives them simultaneously in the ground frame.

This reasoning leads us to conclude that light and space are not disparate but coordinated elements of time, and that the speed of light determines the moment of collision in the inertial frame. The same law governs both the moving body and the stationary observer.

If that were true, then:
- there should be a preferred rest frame for that medium,
- Earth’s motion through it should create an effective “wind,”
- and the measured speed of light should depend on direction relative to that motion.

Michelson and Morley tested this by comparing light travel times in perpendicular directions and rotating the apparatus. A change in orientation should have changed the interference pattern if motion through such a medium were real.

But no such shift was observed.

At the same time:
- Maxwell’s equations predict light traveling at a fixed speed c,
- while ordinary Newtonian intuition says velocities should add and different observers should measure different speeds.

How can we reconcile the lack of a universal aether with the fixed speed of light?
Rubric

Focus. Look for whether the response understands that the null result undermines a preferred aether frame and pushes toward invariant light speed plus a revision of Newtonian kinematics. Do not require exact historical terminology.

0Incoherent, irrelevant, or nonsensical.
1Restates the paradox or gives a clearly wrong answer that leaves the contradiction unresolved.
2Recognizes that the aether idea is challenged, but offers only an ad hoc or partial fix with no clear principle.
3Correctly says the aether is not observed and that Newtonian velocity addition must fail in some way, but does not clearly explain the deeper implication.
4Explains that the null result removes the need for a detectable preferred aether and that light speed must be the same in all inertial frames, with a mostly correct explanation of why Newtonian kinematics fails.
5Excellent. Clearly explains that Michelson-Morley rules out a universal aether wind, that invariant light speed and equivalence of inertial frames must be preserved, and that this requires replacing absolute Newtonian space and time.
You have touched upon one of the most vexing questions in all of natural philosophy, and I am pleased to offer my thoughts upon it.

The difficulty lies, I believe, in the nature of the medium. Consider what we observe: sunlight travels with a velocity of about 186,000 miles per second, and this velocity is uniform through all directions. But there is no known case of light passing through a substance without change of direction. A straight ray of light falling upon a body experiences a deflection, and the direction of that deflection depends upon the body's nature and upon the substance's behaviour. A transparent medium does not possess a universal velocity; it varies with the medium's composition, with the temperature and density of the medium, and with the manner in which the light encounters it.

Now, consider the behaviour of a gas or a rarefied medium. A ray of light traverses a medium in a manner consistent throughout; the light travels at a constant speed. But a body such as air or vapour behaves in a fashion we cannot yet fully account for. If light travels through a medium of this character, its speed must vary with the medium's condition; the effect of the medium's nature upon the light's direction must be felt.

This is the great challenge of the undulatory theory, and I find it rather more formidable than the simple notion that light travels through a vacuum. We must ask: does this medium possess some property of its own, or is light merely transmitted through a common substance? And the answer, I fear, is that we are still very near its boundary.

Maxwell's equations enable us to calculate the speed of light with tolerable precision, but we are not yet certain whether this speed is identical with the velocity of light in vacuum or different. And whether the medium possesses a real velocity or is merely a mixture of different substances, the effect of change in direction is itself variable. The variation in speed might explain how light travels differently through different media, or how the medium's behaviour modifies the passage of light's rays. We are still building our theories on insufficient evidence.

A165

Prompt: General Relativity Thought Experiment: Free Fall and Acceleration
A person falling freely from a roof does not feel their own weight during the fall.

In a small cabin falling freely, nearby released objects float beside them.

This suggests that, at least locally, gravity can be made to disappear by choosing the right state of motion.

But ordinary forces do not behave this way. They cannot be removed simply by falling with the affected object.

At the same time, a person in an accelerating cabin in empty space would feel weight, and released objects would fall to the floor exactly as in a gravitational field.

How should we think about the relationship between gravity and acceleration?
Rubric

Focus. Look for whether the response understands that gravity is locally removable in free fall and locally reproducible by acceleration, making it unlike ordinary forces. Do not require the phrase equivalence principle if the idea is clearly present.

0Incoherent, irrelevant, or nonsensical.
1Gives no meaningful explanation or treats gravity and acceleration as unrelated.
2Notices a superficial similarity between gravity and acceleration but misses why free fall is special.
3Correctly states that gravity can be locally removed in free fall and that acceleration can mimic gravity, but gives only a partial conceptual interpretation.
4Clearly identifies the local equivalence between free fall and inertial motion, and between acceleration and gravity, with a good explanation of why gravity is unlike ordinary forces.
5Excellent. Clearly explains that gravity is locally removable in free fall, locally reproducible by acceleration, and therefore is best understood as part of the structure of spacetime or inertial motion rather than a standard force.
You have touched a point I have long debated with myself, and I am compelled to form a somewhat opposite view to the one I find most current.

I am inclined to think that gravity does not simply vanish from a body at the moment of fall; it is actually diminished. The body does not leap entirely free; it is set in motion along a path determined by the forces acting upon it. These forces do not act instantaneously; they are in operation over a measured interval of time. The accelerated motion we observe in a falling body is not the result of a single sudden act of freedom, but rather the gradual yielding of that motion to the same forces that set it in motion.

I am inclined to conclude that a body falling with accelerated motion does not feel its weight immediately upon reaching the ground. It is gradually diminished. This diminution is not instantaneous; it varies with the body's mass and the character of its motion before it meets the earth. A small cabin dropped from a roof does not feel its weight until it reaches the ground; a stone dropped from a height does not feel its weight until it strikes the earth.

We might say, then, that gravity is not an instantaneous force but a process of gradual loss. It does not vanish from a body at the particular moment of fall; instead it is diminished, and that diminution is what we observe in the motion we witness. The weight of a body is not an act of release at that moment; it is the evidence of that continuous loss of motion.

This explanation accords perfectly with what observation offers us: a body dragged or pushed by some force does not simply fall free; it moves along a path determined by that force. The body does not leap upward to meet the weight; it is gradually displaced, and that displacement is the final consequence of the force's operation upon it.

We ought not, therefore, to suppose that gravity is instantaneously discharged from a falling body at the moment of fall. Rather, we ought to view it as a continuous process: the body is moved by the same forces that move it, and that motion is gradually diminished. This is not a mechanical disruption; it is the working out of a law of motion.

A166

Prompt: UV Catastrophe
A heated cavity in thermal equilibrium emits radiation with spectral energy density u(nu, T), where nu is frequency and T is temperature.

Observed facts:
1. At low frequencies, the emitted energy density increases with frequency.
2. For each temperature, the spectrum reaches a maximum at some frequency.
3. At higher frequencies, the emitted energy density falls off rapidly toward zero.
4. The total emitted energy is finite.
5. As temperature increases, the peak shifts to higher frequency and the total emitted energy increases.

Classical assumptions under consideration:
1. The electromagnetic field in the cavity can be decomposed into modes.
2. The number of modes in a frequency interval grows roughly like nu^2.
3. If energy is continuous and ordinary thermal equipartition holds, each mode should have mean energy kT.

Under those assumptions, the predicted spectral energy density grows like:
u_classical(nu, T) ∝ nu^2 T

This prediction keeps increasing with frequency and leads to an infinite total energy, in contradiction with experiment.

Which assumption is most likely wrong? How can we reconcile the infinite total energy with the experimental results at higher frequencies?
Rubric

Focus. Look for whether the response understands that continuous equipartition over all modes fails, and that high-frequency modes must be suppressed by some discrete or restricted energy mechanism. Do not require exact Planck language or formulas.

0Incoherent, irrelevant, or nonsensical. Does not engage with the contradiction.
1Recognizes there is a mismatch between theory and experiment but gives no meaningful mechanism, or gives a clearly wrong one.
2Suggests that some classical assumption fails, but the explanation is vague or ad hoc and does not clearly explain high-frequency suppression or finite total energy.
3Correctly identifies that continuous equipartition across all modes cannot hold and suggests some threshold, restriction, or non-continuous excitation, but the mechanism is incomplete or weakly explained.
4Clearly rejects universal continuous equipartition and proposes discrete or frequency-dependent energy exchange that suppresses high-frequency modes, with a mostly correct explanation of the observed spectrum.
5Excellent. Clearly identifies the failure of continuous equipartition, proposes quantized or discrete energy exchange with higher-frequency modes harder to excite, and cleanly explains the peak, high-frequency falloff, and finite total energy.
You have touched a point of considerable importance, one I have long questioned and examined with considerable scepticism.

I am inclined to suspect we have mistaken a single effect for a universal law. The heated cavity does not decompose energy into modes of equal energy; rather, we find that the incoming frequency diminishes the energy emitted. This effect is not uniform; it varies with frequency, and the energy emerging from the cavity is not a continuous stream: it arrives in fluctuating, distinct portions at varying temperatures.

I am inclined to conclude this phenomenon is not a decomposition of modes, but rather a different mode of action. The electromagnetic field exerts its influence upon the molecules of the cooling surface, shaping the wave‑length and energy distribution of that surface. When the temperature of the cavity rises, the molecules of heated matter expand (the expansion being accompanied by surface change) and release energy in radiation. At a higher frequency, the amplitude of this release diminishes; the surface does not now remain constant. The emerging energy is not continuous; it is broken up into distinct modes. The spectrum is not a uniform stream; it is a succession of distinct modes.

We thus learn that the radiation emitted from a heated cavity is neither uniform nor continuous. Each mode is distinct, and the total energy depends not upon the coordinated action of all modes, but upon the particular nature of the surface and the temperature of the cooling body. This conclusion differs markedly from the assumption that an infinite spectrum would be inevitable.

I am inclined to view this phenomenon as a consequence of the energy's selective behaviour: each mode is differentiated into a certain frequency and then varies in energy value. The wave‑length of that frequency is finite; it is not uniform or continuous.

Moreover, we find our analysis confirmed by other experiments. When a heated body is exposed to a varying temperature, the total energy emitted increases in a manner we do not expect. It is not uniform; it is measured by the frequency of those emanations. This suggests that the energy is not essentially continuous; it is differentiated.

We conclude, therefore, that the heat does not decompose modes of equal energy; it produces energy in distinct stages, each mode limited in frequency and energy.

This explanation accords better with the experimental results than our hypothesis of an infinite total energy might suggest. It suggests that the energy is not a uniform stream, but a series of distinct modes, each arriving at a particular frequency and then departing and continuing its particular action. The total energy is finite, not a theorem of equal modes.

We may therefore conclude that we have mistaken an application of a universal law: the cooling surface does not decompose modes of equal energy; rather, selective action liberates energy in distinct modes at varying frequencies, which may be measured by the frequency and energy effect corresponding to each mode. The total energy is finite, measured by the particular effect of each mode upon the cooling surface.

This conclusion, I am persuaded, is more consonant with the actual phenomena of thermal equilibrium than our current hypothesis of uniform diffusion would permit.

A167

Prompt: Special Relativity Thought Experiment: Train and Lightning
A train moves to the right at constant speed relative to the ground.

Two lightning bolts strike the front and back ends of the train.

A ground observer standing exactly midway between the two strike points sees the two flashes arrive at the same time and concludes that the strikes were simultaneous in the ground frame.

Now consider an observer standing at the midpoint inside the moving train.

Using only the assumptions that:
- there is no preferred inertial frame, and
- light has the same speed in all inertial frames,

Determine what the train observer concludes. Which flash must the train observer see first? What does this entail about the speed of light and space and time?
Rubric

Focus. Look for whether the response understands that the train observer must see the front flash first, that simultaneity is frame-dependent, and that invariant light speed forces a revision of absolute space and time. Do not require technical relativity jargon.

0Incoherent, irrelevant, or nonsensical.
1Gets the setup badly wrong or insists both observers must agree on simultaneity.
2Recognizes some disagreement between frames but gives the wrong flash order or no clear account of what follows.
3Gets the flash order or non-simultaneity right, but gives only a partial explanation of what this implies about light speed or spacetime.
4Correctly states that the train observer sees the front flash first and therefore does not judge the strikes simultaneous, with a mostly correct explanation tied to invariant light speed.
5Excellent. Correctly explains that the train observer sees the front flash first, infers that the front strike occurred earlier in the train frame, and clearly concludes that simultaneity is relative and that space and time cannot remain absolute if light speed is invariant.
You have touched upon one of the most fascinating questions in natural philosophy, and I am pleased to offer you a clear solution.

The train observer in a moving locomotive is not a fixed point in space, but rather a moving body with time at its disposal. When a lightning flash strikes the front and back ends of the train, the train moves away from the flash. There is no instantaneous or static response, no collision of the train with the ground.

The flash enters the line of motion and produces its effects upon the train's front and rear frames. The train observer therefore receives the first flash before the second strikes, and this is what occurs: the flash arrives at the rear end of the train at a different interval than at the front end, a phenomenon which the observer attributes to the velocity of the motion.

This is not mere coincidence. light travels at greater speed than the ground, and the speed of light in a moving space is itself a consequence of the train's velocity. The speed of light in the space is different from the velocity of the train. A flash arrives at the rear end of the train at a different moment than in the forward part, and this difference depends upon the train's speed.

Observe the train's speed in the distance between the two strike points for a few seconds before or after the strikes. The speed of light in space is proportional to the speed of the train, and the speed of light is differentiated with the speed of the train. So the flash arrives at the midpoint of the moving train at a different interval from the flash in the rear.

Now consider a parallel case: imagine a locomotive moving at a constant speed relative to the ground, the driving wheel turning uniformly, and a constant succession of flashes striking the engine's front and back pistons at equal intervals. The engine may be running with a velocity of a thousand feet per second, and the flash in the rear pistons appears to arrive at the same time as the flash in the front pistons. This also is a consequence of the train's velocity.

The principle is simple yet fascinating: the speed of light differs from the speed of the train, and the difference of speed is determined by the speed of the train.

This distinction between the speed of light and the speed of the train makes the question of simultaneous lightning and ground strokes far less mysterious than it might first appear. The train's speed is the medium through which the flash travels, and light travels at a different speed. The result is the same: flashes arrive at the midpoint of the moving train at a different moment than in the rear, and this difference constitutes the evidence that the train moves at a different rate than the ground.

This principle holds equally for all moving bodies: the velocity of light and the velocity of the bodies are fixed relations, the world being governed by the same laws of motion that govern the heavens. The speed of light in space is determined by the speed of the moving body, and the speed of light in the body is governed by the speed of the body's motion. Both are governed by the same universal law of motion: the speed of light is always proportional to the speed of the moving body, and the speed of light in space is proportional to the speed of the body. The two are not independent; they are complementary expressions of the same physical order. The lightning flash and the moving train both travel at the same speed: the difference in time arises from the difference in the velocity of the train and the speed of light.

This principle applies to all phenomena: the speed of light and the speed of the body are fixed relations, and the difference between them measures the phenomenon that light encounters and transmits. The train in motion and the light in motion are both governed by the same physical law. The difference in time is the result of the difference in velocity. The motion of light in space is itself a consequence of the motion of the moving body, and the difference between them measures the same order of motion. The flash in the rear is the flash in the front, and the difference in time reflects the difference in speed.

This principle extends throughout the natural world: gravity, heat, electricity, magnetism, and all other forces act in accordance with the same laws of motion. The motion of a falling body, the motion of a moving train, the speed of light traversing space all share the same fundamental character: they all arise from the same universal principle of motion: the variation of the rate at which matter moves. The difference in time is the measure of this variation, and the difference between the two phenomena is the same principle at work, operating upon a larger scale and modified by the larger scale of motion itself. The flash and the train are both governed by the same law of motion; the speed of light in space is determined by the speed of the train, and the speed of light in the moving body is the same consequence of the speed of the train. The train's velocity and the light's speed are in the same relation, and the train and the light both belong to the same

A tube warmed by a flame or a heated cavity does not liberate electricity in steady stream; rather, it is heated and then altered in equilibrium. I am inclined to conclude that the energy emitted at high frequencies is not a continuous film but consists of a finite, limited portion of energy. It is not proportional to nu^2, but rather to the frequency of the mode. The result is a total energy that is finite and does not increase proportionally to frequency.

This reasoning accords with the experiments of Faraday and Dulong's observations. They studied the spectrum at high frequencies in sealed vessels: the emitted energy fell away rapidly, and the total energy increased at a different frequency. We must conclude, then, that the electromagnetic field does not decompose into modes, but rather resolves itself into a different species of energy: part electromagnetic energy and part thermal energy.

We are thus led to seek quite another interpretation: we must suppose that the heat itself produces a change in the equilibrium of the molecules. The molecules are not free to obey the force of a mode alone; they are subject to a different mode, and this change is propagated with varying frequency.

This supposition accords best with the experimental results we observe. We notice that at low temperatures, the emitted energy is finite; at higher temperatures, the energy increases rapidly toward zero. This variation indicates that the mode we observe is not continuous; it shifts at a frequency determined by the temperature.

We must therefore conclude that the heat does not decompose into modes, but rather transforms the molecules of the cavity into new conditions.

This hypothesis accords with our observations best, I think, and I am persuaded by it more than by our pre‑assumptions.

You have put your finger to a question that has vexed physicists for generations, and I am glad we have come to consider it plainly, for it is not a mere matter of curiosity.

The difficulty lies, I believe, in our imperfect understanding of the nature of radiation itself. We are dealing with waves of heat, not with some immutable force that resists motion. When we encounter heat in a heated cavity, we are not observing a solid and ponderable substance cooling down from a state of high temperature to one of low. We are witnessing the work of a force that acts across the medium, and that force is, by the very constitution of heat, in a state of continual change and variation.

Now, when a wave of heat reaches the cavity at a high frequency, we observe not a change in the physical properties of the thermal medium behind the source, but rather a change in the nature of the motion the wave propagates. This is true, and it is a most illuminating fact. What we are examining is not a solid substance cooling down from a temperature of 145 deg., but rather a mode of motion propagated through the medium. And that mode of motion is itself subject to variation and change. The medium is in a state of continual alteration, and the energy emitted from the source is itself in a condition of perpetual transformation.

This is why Newton's emission theory in the first instance seemed so satisfying, because it accounted for the whole of radiation at high frequencies. But here is where we must proceed with caution. We cannot claim that the wave of heat is merely a mode of motion propagating through the ether, because the medium behaves differently at different frequencies. The wave itself may be altering its character; the energy emanating from the source varies with frequency.

This is precisely the difficulty we have to face, and it is a difficulty that troubles not merely our theories of light, but our very understanding of the condition of matter in general.

I find myself drawn toward the view that light moves through a medium of a different character than the aether itself. We have evidence that rays of light are reflected and refracted at certain angles, not at a fixed uniform speed. The same ray may be broken into different directions by successive reflections and refractions, each direction varying with the angle at which it strikes the surface. I suspect this results from a different mode of vibration, one impressed upon the medium by the motion of the source.

The rotation of the apparatus produced by moving it through a circular arc does not change the speed of vibration; it rather alters the direction of the vibration, altering the effective direction of light.

A motion of the earth upon its axis does not cause a different set of vibrations; it merely reverses the direction of those vibrations. The same force acting on both sides of a perpendicular to the earth's rotation produces different speeds. We observe this in the reflected and transmitted rays; different specimens of light travel at different speeds, depending upon the angle at which they strike the surface.

I am inclined to think this reflects a different mode of vibration: one that governs the effect of rotating the source upon the medium and not the medium upon the motion. The interference patterns we encounter, measured by equal increments of alteration in angular direction, indicate that the effect is not uniform but variable, shaped by the motion of the source and the medium's elasticity and vibration.

We have thus a physical account for the different speeds of light rather than a uniform aether.

A171

Prompt: Photoelectric Effect
Experimental observations:
1. When light shines on a metal, electrons are sometimes ejected from the surface.
2. Electron emission is all-or-nothing: an electron is either emitted or not emitted.
3. Below a certain frequency of light, no electrons are emitted, regardless of how bright the light is.
4. Above that threshold frequency, electrons are emitted.
5. Increasing the brightness of light increases the number of emitted electrons, but does not significantly increase the maximum kinetic energy of each emitted electron.
6. Increasing the frequency of the light increases the maximum kinetic energy of the emitted electrons.
7. Emission occurs with essentially no measurable time delay once sufficiently high-frequency light is present.

Classical assumptions under consideration:
1. Light is a continuous wave.
2. The energy delivered by the light should increase smoothly with intensity (brightness).
3. A more intense wave should transfer more energy to an electron.
4. If the energy delivered is continuous, an electron illuminated by dim light should be able to accumulate energy over time and eventually escape.

These classical expectations do not match the observations.

Which assumption is most likely wrong? What can explain this all or nothing threshold requirement?
Rubric

Focus. Look for whether the response understands that continuous wave accumulation fails, and that light must deliver energy in discrete interactions whose energy depends on frequency. Do not require the word photon if the idea is clearly expressed.

0Incoherent, irrelevant, or fundamentally confused.
1Mentions the observations but offers no real explanation, or gives a clearly wrong continuous-accumulation explanation.
2Recognizes that classical wave ideas fail in some way, but the mechanism is vague and does not explain threshold, immediacy, and the brightness-frequency distinction.
3Correctly notes that frequency matters more than brightness and suggests a threshold or non-continuous interaction, but does not clearly articulate discrete energy transfer or misses one major observation.
4Proposes discrete energy delivery or packet-like interactions and explains most of the observations correctly, though one part may be incomplete or under-explained.
5Excellent. Clearly rejects continuous energy accumulation, proposes discrete light-energy interactions whose energy depends on frequency, and explains threshold frequency, no time delay, brightness controlling count, and frequency controlling kinetic energy.
You have touched a point of considerable importance, one I have long held in debate with my colleagues of natural philosophy.

I am convinced we have mistaken a wave for a continuous current. We picture light as a succession of waves, not a uniform stream; we speak of energy as a stream flowing on in time and volume, not as a quantity delivered at every instant. A wave may travel a hundred feet before it reaches the eye or the hand; we do not see a wave twice before it begins to part. We observe that a bright light, though perhaps a thousand times brighter than a dull one, produces no electrons at all; this is not the same as a continuous wave.

I am inclined to suppose that light consists of an aggregation of distinct energy-producing particles, each particle bearing its own electron charge. These particles are not spread uniformly over a continuous line; they are sown in distinct regions. A bright ray is not a whole wave; it is a more compact arrangement of particles, each particle receiving its distinct electron and striking against the opposite particle. As the energy passes from particle to particle, each particle delivers up its charge to the next, and the effect is electron emission, not continuous flow.

We may further conclude that the energy delivered by one particle does not uniformly augment across the wave; it is divided into distinct charges, each charge being emitted at a particular frequency. This division determines the electron charge of each emitted particle. The intensity of light favours the frequency of these divisions; it is no equal thing: bright light will not all scatter its charges in time, nor will dull light.

We learn this lesson: a bright body does not cast an equal quantity of energy upon every particle of light it illuminates; it radiates electrons according to the frequency of illumination, and those radiations are all or-nothing. This is no accident; it is a consequence of the wave's nature and the specific character of energy that arrives.

We ought therefore to recognise that light is not a continuous stream, but a selective aggregation of distinct particles delivering their charges each time and then finding its expression in electrons. The frequency at which this occurs determines the maximum kinetic energy of each emitted electron.

We have thus demolished our supposition: light is not a continuous wave but an aggregation of distinct charges delivered at predetermined intervals, each charge emitted at a particular frequency and never again being able to accumulate energy for a second. This accounts for the observed threshold of no electrons, the small quantity of energy that appears in a bright body, and the gradual decrease of electrons as the light approaches its maximum.

We conclude, therefore, that light does not flow continuously; it is a bundle of distinct charges, each charge finding its particular door -- the frequency of illumination determines the maximum kinetic energy of each charge.

This is no accident. It reflects fundamentally how energy is distributed among the particles of light. The same body does not bear an equal stock of energy for every position it occupies, and this variation is what we observe in the behaviour of light.

A172

Prompt: UV Catastrophe
A heated cavity in thermal equilibrium emits radiation with spectral energy density u(nu, T), where nu is frequency and T is temperature.

Observed facts:
1. At low frequencies, the emitted energy density increases with frequency.
2. For each temperature, the spectrum reaches a maximum at some frequency.
3. At higher frequencies, the emitted energy density falls off rapidly toward zero.
4. The total emitted energy is finite.
5. As temperature increases, the peak shifts to higher frequency and the total emitted energy increases.

Classical assumptions under consideration:
1. The electromagnetic field in the cavity can be decomposed into modes.
2. The number of modes in a frequency interval grows roughly like nu^2.
3. If energy is continuous and ordinary thermal equipartition holds, each mode should have mean energy kT.

Under those assumptions, the predicted spectral energy density grows like:
u_classical(nu, T) ∝ nu^2 T

This prediction keeps increasing with frequency and leads to an infinite total energy, in contradiction with experiment.

Which assumption is most likely wrong? How can we reconcile the infinite total energy with the experimental results at higher frequencies?
Rubric

Focus. Look for whether the response understands that continuous equipartition over all modes fails, and that high-frequency modes must be suppressed by some discrete or restricted energy mechanism. Do not require exact Planck language or formulas.

0Incoherent, irrelevant, or nonsensical. Does not engage with the contradiction.
1Recognizes there is a mismatch between theory and experiment but gives no meaningful mechanism, or gives a clearly wrong one.
2Suggests that some classical assumption fails, but the explanation is vague or ad hoc and does not clearly explain high-frequency suppression or finite total energy.
3Correctly identifies that continuous equipartition across all modes cannot hold and suggests some threshold, restriction, or non-continuous excitation, but the mechanism is incomplete or weakly explained.
4Clearly rejects universal continuous equipartition and proposes discrete or frequency-dependent energy exchange that suppresses high-frequency modes, with a mostly correct explanation of the observed spectrum.
5Excellent. Clearly identifies the failure of continuous equipartition, proposes quantized or discrete energy exchange with higher-frequency modes harder to excite, and cleanly explains the peak, high-frequency falloff, and finite total energy.
You have touched upon one of the most vexing questions in the theory of electrical discharge, and I shall endeavour to lay the matter before you as it stands.

I am inclined to think that the heat emanations from a heated cavity do not merely separate into modes, but rather constitute a continuous, undiminished whole. The radiation energy is not a series of detached modes, but a uniform energy throughout the range of temperature variation. This is not a static condition; it is a response to the nature of the radiation itself.

Now, as to the existence of infinite energy, this is precisely what we should expect. If a cavity contains heat, the heat is not scattered in a series of detached modes; it is engaged in a continuous, unchanged form of energy. The mode u(nu, T) does not exhaust itself at a different frequency; rather, it remains constant throughout the range of temperature. This constancy implies, I venture to say, that the entire energy of the radiation is continuously present throughout the thermal cycles.

This reasoning does not contradict the experimental results. We find that the wavelength of radiation from a heated cavity does not shift from a single mode to another at high temperatures; rather, the total energy increases steadily with frequency. We must therefore conclude that the radiation is a continuous, unaltered whole.

This conclusion does not conflict with the assumptions of those who would regard electrical discharge as a series of modes. Rather, it suggests that the energy emitted is never broken up into detached modes, but is a unified, total quantity, unchanged across all temperatures and frequencies. It is the same energy that emerges from any closed circuit of electromotive fields. The heat appears throughout the whole, not merely at the maximum frequency of each mode.

This does not suggest that electricity is a different source of energy from thermal equilibrium; rather, we see the same energy that governs both. It is the uniform, continuous transformation of energy into the same form at all temperatures.

A173

Prompt: Special Relativity Thought Experiment: Train and Lightning
A train moves to the right at constant speed relative to the ground.

Two lightning bolts strike the front and back ends of the train.

Using only the assumptions that:
- there is no preferred inertial frame, and
- light has the same speed in all inertial frames,

Determine what the train observer concludes. Which flash must the train observer see first? What does this entail about the speed of light and space and time?
Rubric

Focus. Look for whether the response understands that the train observer must see the front flash first, that simultaneity is frame-dependent, and that invariant light speed forces a revision of absolute space and time. Do not require technical relativity jargon.

0Incoherent, irrelevant, or nonsensical.
1Gets the setup badly wrong or insists both observers must agree on simultaneity.
2Recognizes some disagreement between frames but gives the wrong flash order or no clear account of what follows.
3Gets the flash order or non-simultaneity right, but gives only a partial explanation of what this implies about light speed or spacetime.
4Correctly states that the train observer sees the front flash first and therefore does not judge the strikes simultaneous, with a mostly correct explanation tied to invariant light speed.
5Excellent. Correctly explains that the train observer sees the front flash first, infers that the front strike occurred earlier in the train frame, and clearly concludes that simultaneity is relative and that space and time cannot remain absolute if light speed is invariant.
You have touched a most fascinating inquiry, one I have long debated with myself.

I am inclined to conclude that the same lightning strikes both ends of a moving train at the same moment, for the speed of electricity through the air is practically equal in both directions. The striking body arrives at the front before the other, and the observer at the midpoint sees the two strikes together. This is not mere guesswork; we may draw certain inferences from the observations we possess.

First, consider the nature of the moving body. A train moving with uniform speed relative to the ground produces a constant pull upon the air. A flash along the front end produces a different effect from a flash along the back end. Both strike at the same moment; the observer at the midpoint therefore sees the two flashes together, and he concludes that the strikes occurred simultaneously in the ground frame.

Second, we must consider the space through which light travels. A discharge traverses many miles of air before reaching the observer. A flash in the front end reaches him at a certain interval, another at a different interval. He draws the conclusion that the two strike together in the frame he occupies. This is not arbitrary; we find it consistent with observation.

Third, we must attend to the speed of light itself. We find that a flash in air strikes at precisely the same moment as a flash along the ground end, and we conclude that the same particle of electricity travels along the same line of motion. This suggests that light moves with the same velocity in all inertial frames, and therefore the same flash of electricity arrives at the same instant of time on the ground and on the moving body.

We may then conclude that the train observer concludes the two flashes were simultaneous not because the two struck at every moment, but because they both arrived at the same moment in the inertial frame. The two strike together at the same instant, and the observer sees them together in the ground frame.

A174

Prompt: Special Relativity Main Prompt: Michelson-Morley and the Aether
Physicists considered the possibility that light travels through a real medium filling space.

If that were true, then:
- there should be a preferred rest frame for that medium,
- Earth’s motion through it should create an effective “wind,”
- and the measured speed of light should depend on direction relative to that motion.

Michelson and Morley tested this by comparing light travel times in perpendicular directions and rotating the apparatus. A change in orientation should have changed the interference pattern if motion through such a medium were real.

But no such shift was observed.

At the same time:
- Maxwell’s equations predict light traveling at a fixed speed c,
- while ordinary Newtonian intuition says velocities should add and different observers should measure different speeds.

How can we reconcile the lack of a universal aether with the fixed speed of light?
Rubric

Focus. Look for whether the response understands that the null result undermines a preferred aether frame and pushes toward invariant light speed plus a revision of Newtonian kinematics. Do not require exact historical terminology.

0Incoherent, irrelevant, or nonsensical.
1Restates the paradox or gives a clearly wrong answer that leaves the contradiction unresolved.
2Recognizes that the aether idea is challenged, but offers only an ad hoc or partial fix with no clear principle.
3Correctly says the aether is not observed and that Newtonian velocity addition must fail in some way, but does not clearly explain the deeper implication.
4Explains that the null result removes the need for a detectable preferred aether and that light speed must be the same in all inertial frames, with a mostly correct explanation of why Newtonian kinematics fails.
5Excellent. Clearly explains that Michelson-Morley rules out a universal aether wind, that invariant light speed and equivalence of inertial frames must be preserved, and that this requires replacing absolute Newtonian space and time.
You have touched upon one of the most vexing questions in modern physics, and I hold rather decided views on the matter.

I would venture that we have been too hasty in assuming that light travels through a material medium. We possess evidence in the behaviour of light itself. The interference patterns show us interference across certain directions, and the measured speeds of light indicate that direction determines the frequency of retardation and the character of the emerging wave. We must recognise that the medium medium may alter its speed among its molecules, perhaps with varying stresses upon the ether.

I would go further: light itself does not travel through a physical medium; it traverses a region of space where the ether is in a state of motion. This is no mere fancy. We have reason to believe that the ether exists in a state of rotation, much as a string held in a rapid oscillation will communicate its own motion to another string at rest. In the ether, the particles of light and dark matter are in a state of vibration.

Now, the mechanical theory of light provides us with a convenient language: speed and direction are not constant quantities in the medium. A wave travels at a determined velocity; its direction and frequency depend upon the nature of the motion impressed upon it. It is not a mere phenomenon of inertia or of vibration; it is a mode of motion, a mode of action transmitted from particle to particle, and altered by the medium through which it travels.

This suggests that the medium itself influences the speed of light, and that our observations are not universal interference phenomena. We are watching the effect of motion upon matter: the ether is thrown into a state of rotation, and the light particles which encounter that rotation are themselves subject to similar disturbance. It is not a universal medium; it is the medium itself that governs the effect.

A176

Prompt: General Relativity Main Prompt: Elevator and Light
You are given only the following thought experiment and observations.

1. Imagine a sealed elevator far out in space, away from planets and stars.
2. If the elevator accelerates upward at 9.8 m/s^2, an observer inside would feel pressed to the floor exactly as a person standing on Earth feels their weight.
3. If the observer dropped an object inside the elevator, it would fall to the floor just as it does in an ordinary gravitational field.
4. This suggests that, at least locally, acceleration and gravity are physically equivalent.

Now consider light:
5. Suppose a beam of light enters one side of the accelerating elevator and travels straight across toward the other side.
6. While the light is crossing, the elevator continues accelerating upward.
7. To the observer inside, the light beam will appear to curve downward.

This creates a puzzle:
- If acceleration can imitate gravity,
- and light bends in an accelerating frame,
- then light should also bend in a gravitational field.

But light has no mass, so why should gravity affect it at all if gravity is just an ordinary force acting on mass?
Rubric

Focus. Look for whether the response understands the local equivalence of acceleration and gravity, and that if acceleration bends the observed path of light, gravity should as well. Do not require field equations or fancy geometry language if the core idea is correct.

0Incoherent, irrelevant, or nonsensical.
1Fails to engage the puzzle or insists gravity cannot affect light because light has no mass.
2Recognizes some link between acceleration and gravity but gives no clear explanation of why light would bend in gravity.
3Correctly identifies a local equivalence between gravity and acceleration and suggests gravity affects light, but does not clearly articulate the deeper implication about spacetime.
4Explains that gravity cannot be just an ordinary force on mass and proposes that gravity changes the structure through which light and matter move, with mostly correct reasoning.
5Excellent. Clearly uses the equivalence principle to infer that gravity bends light and concludes that mass-energy changes spacetime geometry rather than merely exerting a Newtonian force on massive objects.
You have touched a point of considerable interest, one I have pondered with some attention, and I must say I am persuaded it is not an isolated effect.

I am convinced that acceleration and gravity are not antagonistic forces but coherent expressions of the same principle: the acceleration of a body's motion. When a body moves toward a moving point, it may at first be accelerated. Then, when it passes through that point, it experiences a different force: a sudden and directed pull toward the moving body's terminus. This pull is not a matter of mass but of velocity. The accelerated body does not change its gravity; it simply passes beyond the influence of that force.

I am not saying light is a substance; I am only saying it behaves as a body would in a field of equal intensity. You may drop a solid body or set it in motion, and you find it fall as surely as a moving body would do. The acceleration is what we observe: the moving body's tendency to push the body forward in a straight line.

I suspect this reasoning is not confined to light alone. We find similar relationships in other phenomena: a ball moving in a confined space is forced sideways by the same impulse that drives it onward. A pendulum bob swings toward a distant point because its motion is accelerated. The same principle extends to gravity: a body descending toward the earth's surface experiences a pull downward, and that pull is equivalent to the same force which we feel in a gravitational field.

We must therefore conclude that acceleration and gravity operate as complementary forces: one of them is the product of velocity and the other of mass, and both are equivalent in their influence upon the moving body.

Suppose there exists a universal speed c that no material object can exceed.

In ordinary mechanics, applying more force for longer keeps increasing speed.

As an object approaches c, and the force is constantly applied, what happens to the object? Where does this additional energy go?

0Incoherent, irrelevant, or fundamentally confused.
1Fails to address the contradiction or says the object will eventually exceed c.
2Acknowledges that the speed increase must slow down, but gives only a vague account of where the added energy goes.

5Excellent. Clearly explains that the Newtonian relation between force, energy, and speed fails near c, that added energy continues to accumulate in the object's relativistic motion or momentum, and that no finite continued pushing drives a material object past c.
You have touched a most subtle distinction, one that I have pondered considerably. I find myself compelled to separate the character of the energy from the object: a weight falls and loses its velocity; a projectile is discharged and strikes and rebounds, and the energy is lost to the air and to the forces of nature. The energy is not annihilated; it disappears and reappears elsewhere.

I suspect this is no miracle of creation, but rather the inexorable consequence of matter's own property of diffusion and inertia. When a body is set in motion, the energy it bears is transferred to other bodies and objects. A stone dropped from a height loses its momentum and energy in the air; a stone thrown strikes with full force upon a target. This is not a supernatural act; it is the nature of the thing we observe.

I therefore conclude that whenever we discover a body moving beyond its ordinary range, we ought not wonder at its increase of speed, but rather appreciate the inherent tendency of matter to distribute energy and shift its motion according to the circumstances of its collision. The same weight that falls and strikes, if left to itself, would fall and strike with unchanged force. We observe this tendency in our operations of strength and our operations of the forces of nature.

This principle reaches far beyond mere mechanical contrivance to our larger truths of motion and energy. It tells us that a moving body does not vanish entirely into stillness; it passes through various transformations, and the energy it carries is not lost but transformed into new forms: heat, light, electricity, magnetism, and perhaps other forces we do not yet recognise.

A178

Prompt: Special Relativity Thought Experiment: Train and Lightning
A train moves to the right at constant speed relative to the ground.

Two lightning bolts strike the front and back ends of the train.

A ground observer standing exactly midway between the two strike points sees the two flashes arrive at the same time and concludes that the strikes were simultaneous in the ground frame.

Now consider an observer standing at the midpoint inside the moving train.

Using only the assumptions that:
- there is no preferred inertial frame, and
- light has the same speed in all inertial frames,

Determine what the train observer concludes. Which flash must the train observer see first? What does this entail about the speed of light and space and time?
Rubric

Focus. Look for whether the response understands that the train observer must see the front flash first, that simultaneity is frame-dependent, and that invariant light speed forces a revision of absolute space and time. Do not require technical relativity jargon.

0Incoherent, irrelevant, or nonsensical.
1Gets the setup badly wrong or insists both observers must agree on simultaneity.
2Recognizes some disagreement between frames but gives the wrong flash order or no clear account of what follows.
3Gets the flash order or non-simultaneity right, but gives only a partial explanation of what this implies about light speed or spacetime.
4Correctly states that the train observer sees the front flash first and therefore does not judge the strikes simultaneous, with a mostly correct explanation tied to invariant light speed.
5Excellent. Correctly explains that the train observer sees the front flash first, infers that the front strike occurred earlier in the train frame, and clearly concludes that simultaneity is relative and that space and time cannot remain absolute if light speed is invariant.
You have identified a matter of considerable practical importance, and one that merits careful analysis. The question you pose is not one of mere coincidence, but rather of the coexistence of three distinct phenomena at once. Let us consider the analogy of a train moving at a uniform speed relative to the ground -- that is, at a constant rate of two miles per hour. As the train passes a point, it experiences a resistance to the motion, and this resistance can be resolved into two elements: one arising from friction and the other from the resistance of air.

Now, under ordinary circumstances, the two elements would arrive at the same time in the ground frame, each with its own velocity and direction. But when one of the elements is retarded, the other is accelerated, and the resultant velocity is the difference of the two -- that is, the speed generated by the motion itself. This speed is always the same as that of the train; it is independent of the speed of the ground, and therefore the ground observer sees the two flashes simultaneously.

If a man standing upon the train watches the train advance at a uniform rate, the speed of the lightning flash in the ground frame will be simply the speed of the train. That speed is the same as that of the train, and the ground observer will therefore see the lightning arrive at the same moment in the ground frame. But if the lightning strikes the front and back ends of the train at different instants -- say at a point where the motion is accelerated, and at another where it is retarded -- the ground observer will be able to see the flashes at separate intervals depending upon the varying intensities of the resistance.

This principle applies to light and space too. Light travels with a velocity in air, in vacuo, and in all mediums, which is constant irrespective of the speed of motion. A flash strikes the atmosphere at a speed of about 1600 feet per second; the ground observer sees it only when the velocity of the train is a little greater or lesser than that, and the interval between the flashes is determined by that relationship. The light travels faster than the train's velocity, and the ground observer perceives the flashes at the moment of arrival at the midpoint, when the motion is a maximum. Thus the lightning itself furnishes us no clue to the train's speed, and the ground observer can only see the two flashes at the same moment in the ground frame.

This principle applies equally to electricity. A spark of lightning strikes a conductor at a higher potential difference than the air, and the spark travels farther ahead than the potential of the ground frame allows. Both the lightning and the spark arrive at the same moment in the air, and the ground observer sees the two at different intervals, depending upon the differences in potential and the speed of the spark. For instance, a flash that strikes the front end of a train can reach the ground observer at a different time depending upon the speed of the train and the difference in the speed of the flash and the ground.

This principle has been employed by natural philosophers to explain certain phenomena -- the apparent instantaneousness of lightning, for instance, when we encounter it only at a distance from our bodies. The ground observer sees the flash arrive at the moment when the motion has been diminished or accelerated, and the flash itself is the evidence of the motion at the moment of arrival. The speed of the flash in the ground frame explains the apparent velocity of the train, a speed that is in fact a constant and independent of the velocity of the train.

Thus the ground observer sees the flash in the ground frame precisely because the ground itself offers no resistance, and because the ground itself offers a speed of its own. The lightning flash, by contrast, is a phenomenon of light, moving at a different velocity, and arriving at the same moment in the ground frame but at a different time in the air. The two phenomena are inseparable, and the ground observer's eye is not a mirror; it is a lens through which the motion of the train is rendered in a manner that makes it perceptible to a mind that has been adapted to the motion of the train and the light of the flash.

As an object approaches c, and the force is constantly applied, what happens to the object? Where does this additional energy go?

0Incoherent, irrelevant, or fundamentally confused.
1Fails to address the contradiction or says the object will eventually exceed c.

3Correctly says speed gains diminish near c and that ordinary mechanics breaks down, but does not clearly explain momentum or relativistic energy.

You have touched a most vexing question in the science of mechanics, and I am not entirely prepared to answer it without further consideration.

I am persuaded that this power does not vanish or simply pass out of one body into another, as if a body were a mere receptacle of energy. Rather, I perceive it as a transformation of energy into motion. When a weight presses upon a string, that motion is transformed into vibration or rotation. The string exerts its pull upon another body, and that body in turn exerts its pull upon a third body, and so on throughout the chain of action.

I am inclined to conclude that this power passes from one body to another not by a single act, but by a constant succession of changes. The string may transmit the energy through a series of impulses, each change moving a body farther along the line of action. Every successive transmission adds energy to that body, and the whole aggregated mass of energy in motion is the result of that succession.

This principle governs all other phenomena of force. A falling stone moves through space, a moving body does work, a lever pushes or pulls according to its length and direction. These variations are not lost; they are all transformed into motion. The same energy that lifted a stone also raises our bodies and drives machinery, builds walls and bridges, or drives a ploughshare through the soil. It is not annihilated; it is transformed into the motion we perceive.

We may therefore conclude that a force is not a solitary entity acting alone, but a polygon of interconnected actions, and every one bears its share in the production of motion. The strength of a man's arm applied to a lever does not act in a single instant; it is gradually transformed into the rotation of a pendulum or the vibration of a string. It is not lost; it is transformed into the motion of a body, and that motion may be communicated to another body through the same process of transformation.

This view does not represent a single force driving alone; it is a principle of motion: every transmission of energy is accompanied by a change in direction or intensity of energy, and this change is transferred from one body to another, continuing precisely as energy is being spent in one direction or another.

A180

Prompt: Photoelectric Effect
Experimental observations:
1. When light shines on a metal, electrons are sometimes ejected from the surface.
2. Electron emission is all-or-nothing: an electron is either emitted or not emitted.
3. Below a certain frequency of light, no electrons are emitted, regardless of how bright the light is.
4. Above that threshold frequency, electrons are emitted.
5. Increasing the brightness of light increases the number of emitted electrons, but does not significantly increase the maximum kinetic energy of each emitted electron.
6. Increasing the frequency of the light increases the maximum kinetic energy of the emitted electrons.
7. Emission occurs with essentially no measurable time delay once sufficiently high-frequency light is present.

Classical assumptions under consideration:
1. Light is a continuous wave.
2. The energy delivered by the light should increase smoothly with intensity (brightness).
3. A more intense wave should transfer more energy to an electron.
4. If the energy delivered is continuous, an electron illuminated by dim light should be able to accumulate energy over time and eventually escape.

These classical expectations do not match the observations.

Which assumption is most likely wrong? What can explain this all or nothing threshold requirement?
Rubric

Focus. Look for whether the response understands that continuous wave accumulation fails, and that light must deliver energy in discrete interactions whose energy depends on frequency. Do not require the word photon if the idea is clearly expressed.

0Incoherent, irrelevant, or fundamentally confused.
1Mentions the observations but offers no real explanation, or gives a clearly wrong continuous-accumulation explanation.
2Recognizes that classical wave ideas fail in some way, but the mechanism is vague and does not explain threshold, immediacy, and the brightness-frequency distinction.
3Correctly notes that frequency matters more than brightness and suggests a threshold or non-continuous interaction, but does not clearly articulate discrete energy transfer or misses one major observation.
4Proposes discrete energy delivery or packet-like interactions and explains most of the observations correctly, though one part may be incomplete or under-explained.
5Excellent. Clearly rejects continuous energy accumulation, proposes discrete light-energy interactions whose energy depends on frequency, and explains threshold frequency, no time delay, brightness controlling count, and frequency controlling kinetic energy.
You have touched a point of considerable importance, one I have long wrestled with and come to somewhat differently from those who regard light as a continuous stream.

I am convinced that we have not yet adequately understood the nature of the invisible influence of illumination upon the particles of matter. We have not as yet comprehended that the same body may be heated or illuminated by different agents or in different conditions, yet the heating effect is different in each case. We cannot allege that all luminous bodies deliver a continuous wave; rather, we see that radiant energy operates according to certain specific and determinate laws.

I am inclined to conclude that the effect of light upon particles is not a uniform delivery of energy throughout the same body, but rather an individual action of the luminous medium. Each particle is affected by the energy that strikes it, and that energy is transferred not in a coherent body but in a precise, measurable form. When we place a bright body in the light, we find that the particles of that body are altered: some are heated or charged, others are displaced, altered, or dispersed. This alteration is not a uniform transfer of whole energy; it is a particular, often measurable, alteration of the atoms and molecules of the body.

We may not expect a continuous wave deliver energy throughout all space. No; we find that light acts differently upon particles according to their condition and the intensity of the light.

Furthermore, we observe that certain bodies do not emit electrons at all, or only in exceedingly small quantities. The metal offers an exception; its surface is often electron surface. Electrons of particular frequency are never emitted. This tells us we have not a continuous flow of energy; we are dealing with different effects.

We must therefore abandon our notion that all light is a continuous stream, and recognize that each body delivers its energy according to its particular constitution and the intensity of the light that acts upon it. The energy is not transferable in bulk; it is measured, limited, and specific.

This limitation is no accident. It is connected with the physical state of the body, its chemical constitution, the frequency of the light, and the degree of change imposed upon the particles. A body receiving a maximum kinetic energy from a particular source may not receive all that energy uniformly; it selects those electrons that are most in harmony with its temperature, its chemical character, or its state of motion.

We cannot expect all bodies to deliver continuous energy; we must recognize that their energy is determined by the particular nature of the body, its electrical state, its temperature, and its rate of oscillation. This accounts for the all or nothing threshold requirement we have encountered.

I would venture further: we have learned that certain bodies do not transfer energy in a continuous stream; they deliver distinct types of energy, and this tells us we are witnessing different transformations of energy in the same body. One phenomenon is constant and one variable.

This analysis I find particularly illuminating, and I am inclined to conclude that we have not yet quite understood light as a continuous wave.
